English for Work · Health & life sciences

Medical Assisting English

Clarify visit details, distinguish reported symptoms from observations, reconcile information for clinician review, track referrals, and handle patient questions with accurate, respectful English.

8 practical lessonsIntermediate to advanced1 complete learner book
Start practicing →

Words into action

Read the situation.
Find your words.
Make yourself clear.

For Medical assistants and outpatient clinic support staff.

Open learner book (PDF) ↗

Copy complete AI prompts ↓

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Clarify a mismatch between a scheduled visit and a patient concern while respecting the preferred name.
  • Give a concise patient report that separates the patient words, observed behavior, and unassessed clinical questions.
  • Document a reported medication change without treating an unverified history as a new clinical instruction.
  • Preserve collection and arrival times accurately when a colleague proposes an unnecessary record change.
  • Explain referral stages and a named follow-up date without accidentally confirming an appointment.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

Your eight lessons

Open the learner book (PDF) ↗
01 Clarifying the visit at check-in02 Rooming and factual patient reports03 Medication histories with clear sources04 Laboratory paperwork and specimen queries05 Referrals and appointment dependencies06 Supporting questions during a clinical visit07 Telephone concerns and clinical escalation08 Records, corrections, and follow-up ownership
Prompts

All patients, records, times, and cases are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not clinical assessment, triage, prescribing, specimen procedures, legal advice, or professional certification. Duties depend on local law, training, delegation, supervision, and clinic policy. Follow actual clinical and emergency procedures; these dialogues do not establish that any symptom can safely wait. US sources provide context, not universal rules. Use actual dates and required identifiers in real records.

01Clarifying the visit at check-inThe schedule and the expectation+

Your goal

Clarify a mismatch between a scheduled visit and a patient concern while respecting the preferred name.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

The schedule and the expectation

The schedule lists an annual review for patient Alexandra, whose verified record has already been matched through the clinic process. The patient prefers Alex and expected to discuss a recent concern. Assistant Sam cannot decide whether the appointment type is suitable. Nurse Dana is available to clarify the visit plan. No decision about the concern, billing, urgency, or an additional appointment has been supplied. Sam must acknowledge the expectation, preserve accurate identification, use Alex in conversation, and ask Dana to clarify the clinical plan.

Alex: Patient / Sam: Medical assistant

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does the schedule show?

Answer and explanation

A. An annual review

The schedule lists an annual review, while the patient's expectation differs.

2. What should Sam do with the name preference?

Answer and explanation

D. Use Alex while preserving the verified record match.

Alex is the preferred spoken name; that does not justify a duplicate record or an unverified identifier change.

3. Who can clarify the visit plan?

Answer and explanation

C. Nurse Dana

Dana is the available clinical route, and Sam cannot determine suitability independently.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

An appointment label describes a scheduled category, not a clinical judgment about every concern raised that day. Do not promise that the concern will be resolved, reject it automatically, or invent a billing outcome from the schedule alone.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
check-in
Arrival process confirming a patient and the scheduled visit.complete check-in
appointment type
Category describing the scheduled purpose or format of a visit.verify the appointment type
annual review
Periodic review visit recorded as occurring yearly.attend an annual review
presenting concern
Issue the patient brings to the current visit.record the presenting concern
chief complaint
Clinical-record term for the main reason for seeking care.clarify the chief complaint
visit agenda
Topics planned for discussion during the encounter.clarify the visit agenda
preferred name
Name a person asks others to use when addressing them.use the preferred name
recorded name
Name shown in the relevant patient record.verify the recorded name
patient identifier
Information used to match a person with the correct record.confirm patient identifiers
demographic details
Administrative identity and contact information in a record.verify demographic details
registration
Creation or updating of required administrative patient information.complete registration
encounter
A particular interaction or visit involving healthcare services.document the encounter
appointment slot
Reserved time in a schedule.confirm the appointment slot
scheduled purpose
Reason for a visit as recorded in the booking.state the scheduled purpose
patient expectation
What a patient believes will happen during a service or visit.acknowledge the patient expectation
clinical suitability
Whether a plan or appointment is appropriate for the clinical need.refer a suitability question
scope of practice
Activities permitted within a role under applicable requirements.work within scope of practice
delegation
Assignment of an activity under the applicable professional rules.clarify the delegation
supervision
Required oversight of work by an appropriate professional.confirm supervision arrangements
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant information to a receiving clinical professional.complete a clinical handoff
visit plan
Intended approach to the current appointment.clarify the visit plan
billing category
Classification used for charging or reimbursement purposes.verify the billing category
identity match
Confirmation that a person corresponds to the correct record.establish the identity match
name pronunciation
The way a person's name is spoken.check name pronunciation

Using Alex does not mean silently replacing verified identifiers or creating another record. Follow the clinic process for recorded preferences and corrections. Respectful address supports accurate identification; it does not remove the need for it.

C Notice the language

Respect the person and verify the record

A preferred name and a verified record name can coexist. Use the requested form of address while following the actual identification process. A mismatch in visit expectations deserves clarification rather than blame for booking or an automatic refusal to discuss the concern.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Use the preferred name
Thank you, Alex; I will use that name.
State the schedule
The schedule lists an annual review.
Acknowledge the expectation
You expected to discuss a recent concern.
Name the difference
The recorded visit type and your expectation are not the same.
Avoid a clinical decision
I cannot decide whether this appointment type meets that need.
Offer a named route
Nurse Dana can clarify the visit plan.
Preserve identification
We will keep the verified record linked correctly.
Avoid blame
Let us clarify the plan before assuming how the mismatch happened.
Separate billing
I cannot determine charges from this appointment label alone.
Keep the concern visible
I will include your stated concern in the handoff.
Protect privacy
We can use the appropriate setting for personal details.
Do not promise treatment
I cannot promise a particular clinical outcome.
Ask for clarification
Dana, can you clarify the visit plan with Alex?
Confirm the preference
The verified record says Alexandra; the preferred spoken name is Alex.
Avoid duplicate records
We should use the correctly matched existing record.
Close the loop
I will explain the mismatch to Dana so you do not have to start from nothing.
6 language and precision notes
Lists
Attributes the category to the schedule rather than presenting it as a clinical decision.
You expected
Acknowledges the patient's understanding without assigning fault.
Can clarify
Offers a next step without promising a clinical outcome.
Before assuming
Stops blame from replacing a factual check.
Linked correctly
Keeps respectful address and accurate identification together.
From the label alone
Limits conclusions about billing or suitability.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which handoff preserves both facts?

Answer and explanation

A. The schedule says annual review; Alex expected to discuss a recent concern.

The statement preserves the scheduled category and reported expectation without inventing fault or a clinical decision.

2. What does using Alex change here?

Answer and explanation

B. The respectful form of spoken address, not the verified identity match

The preferred name affects address while record matching and other decisions remain separate.

3. Which reassurance is unsupported?

Answer and explanation

C. This visit will definitely resolve the concern at no additional cost.

Neither the clinical outcome nor any billing decision is established by the facts.

4. Which action is appropriate?

Answer and explanation

A. Pass the mismatch to Dana while keeping the correctly matched record.

The available route is clinical clarification with accurate identification, not an unauthorized record or treatment change.

Check explanations in the book, page 85 ↗

E Conversations

The schedule and the expectation

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Alex: Patient / Sam: Medical assistant

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • identity match
  • clinical handoff
  • presenting concern
  • appointment type
  • preferred name
  • visit plan
  • billing category
  • scheduled purpose
  • clinical suitability
  • patient expectation
  1. Alex
    I am here for my appointment, but I noticed the reminder says annual review. I thought this was the visit for the concern I called about.
  2. Sam
    The 1 is an annual review. Thank you for explaining what you expected; I will ask nurse Dana to clarify the plan.
  3. Alex
    Before we go further, please call me Alex. Alexandra is the name in my records, but I use Alex in conversation and at work.
  4. Sam
    Of course, Alex. Your 2 is clear, and we will keep the verified record correctly linked. I am not creating another record because you use a shorter name.
  5. Alex
    Good. I worry that the appointment category means nobody will hear the reason I actually came. Do I need to book something else?
  6. Sam
    I cannot decide 3 from the schedule label. Dana can clarify how the visit should address your concern before we assume that another booking is required.
  7. Alex
    I do not want to explain personal details here in front of everyone. Can the question reach Dana without a discussion across reception?
  8. Sam
    Yes. I can identify the mismatch and use the appropriate private route for the 4. We do not need to announce personal details to the waiting room.
  9. Alex
    Will you tell Dana that I expected this to be the concern visit, rather than just saying that I am unhappy about the reminder?
  10. Sam
    I will preserve that 5. The handoff will say the schedule lists an annual review and you expected a discussion of a recent concern.
  11. Alex
    Is this a billing problem too? I have heard different visit categories can mean different charges, and I do not want a surprise.
  12. Sam
    A 6 is another question to clarify through the proper process. I cannot promise a charge or coverage outcome from the label alone.
  13. Alex
    Please check before anyone promises it is covered. I took time off for this visit, and I need to understand what will actually happen today.
  14. Sam
    I can confirm the schedule and your expectation, then request a 7 to Dana. I cannot promise how the clinician will resolve the concern.
  15. Alex
    Please also make sure the team uses Alex. Sometimes one person agrees, but the preference disappears as soon as I move to another room.
  16. Sam
    I will handle that preference through our record process while preserving the 8. The name used in conversation and the correctly matched record need not conflict.
  17. Alex
    Thank you. I do not need you to change the appointment yourself; I need someone qualified to clarify what today's visit can include.
  18. Sam
    That is the 9 question for Dana. I will not turn the appointment label into a refusal or a guarantee before that discussion.
  19. Alex
    All right. Can you let Dana know I am here? I would like to settle the visit plan before I explain everything again.
  20. Sam
    Correct. I will record the 10 accurately and pass on your expectation. We will keep the two facts clear while Dana clarifies the next step.

Answers and explanations
  1. scheduled purpose Turn 2. The scheduled purpose is the annual review recorded in the booking, which differs from Alex's stated expectation.
  2. preferred name Turn 4. The preferred name is Alex, while the verified record name remains Alexandra and must stay correctly linked.
  3. clinical suitability Turn 6. Clinical suitability requires the appropriate clinical judgment; Sam cannot decide it from the appointment label alone.
  4. presenting concern Turn 8. The presenting concern is what Alex wants addressed, and it should reach the clinical team without unnecessary public disclosure.
  5. patient expectation Turn 10. Patient expectation describes what Alex believed the visit would address, not merely dissatisfaction with an administrative reminder.
  6. billing category Turn 12. A billing category may affect charging, but the scenario supplies no verified charge or coverage decision from the visit label.
  7. clinical handoff Turn 14. The clinical handoff passes the mismatch to Dana, the identified clinical route, without Sam inventing a treatment decision.
  8. identity match Turn 16. The identity match links Alex to the verified existing record and must remain intact while the preferred address is communicated.
  9. visit plan Turn 18. The visit plan concerns what this encounter will address and is the question Dana can clarify with Alex.
  10. appointment type Turn 20. The appointment type remains the recorded annual review, distinct from the clinical clarification now requested.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Alex: I am here for my appointment, but I noticed the reminder says annual review. I thought this was the visit for the concern I called about.
  2. Sam: The scheduled purpose is an annual review. Thank you for explaining what you expected; I will ask nurse Dana to clarify the plan.
  3. Alex: Before we go further, please call me Alex. Alexandra is the name in my records, but I use Alex in conversation and at work.
  4. Sam: Of course, Alex. Your preferred name is clear, and we will keep the verified record correctly linked. I am not creating another record because you use a shorter name.
  5. Alex: Good. I worry that the appointment category means nobody will hear the reason I actually came. Do I need to book something else?
  6. Sam: I cannot decide clinical suitability from the schedule label. Dana can clarify how the visit should address your concern before we assume that another booking is required.
  7. Alex: I do not want to explain personal details here in front of everyone. Can the question reach Dana without a discussion across reception?
  8. Sam: Yes. I can identify the mismatch and use the appropriate private route for the presenting concern. We do not need to announce personal details to the waiting room.
  9. Alex: Will you tell Dana that I expected this to be the concern visit, rather than just saying that I am unhappy about the reminder?
  10. Sam: I will preserve that patient expectation. The handoff will say the schedule lists an annual review and you expected a discussion of a recent concern.
  11. Alex: Is this a billing problem too? I have heard different visit categories can mean different charges, and I do not want a surprise.
  12. Sam: A billing category is another question to clarify through the proper process. I cannot promise a charge or coverage outcome from the label alone.
  13. Alex: Please check before anyone promises it is covered. I took time off for this visit, and I need to understand what will actually happen today.
  14. Sam: I can confirm the schedule and your expectation, then request a clinical handoff to Dana. I cannot promise how the clinician will resolve the concern.
  15. Alex: Please also make sure the team uses Alex. Sometimes one person agrees, but the preference disappears as soon as I move to another room.
  16. Sam: I will handle that preference through our record process while preserving the identity match. The name used in conversation and the correctly matched record need not conflict.
  17. Alex: Thank you. I do not need you to change the appointment yourself; I need someone qualified to clarify what today's visit can include.
  18. Sam: That is the visit plan question for Dana. I will not turn the appointment label into a refusal or a guarantee before that discussion.
  19. Alex: All right. Can you let Dana know I am here? I would like to settle the visit plan before I explain everything again.
  20. Sam: Correct. I will record the appointment type accurately and pass on your expectation. We will keep the two facts clear while Dana clarifies the next step.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Alex's visit expectation

Fictional check-in using the lesson case.

  1. Assistant: Alex, today's booking says annual review. What chief concern did you expect to discuss?
  2. Patient: Not exactly. I expected to discuss a recent concern, and I'm worried the booking is wrong.
  3. Assistant: I'll tell Dana about that difference so she can clarify the visit plan.
  4. Patient: Please don't assume I came for the wrong reason just because of the schedule label.
  5. Assistant: I won't. What would you like me to say is your main concern today?
  6. Patient: I'd like the clinician to hear about a change I noticed, rather than just review paperwork.
  7. Assistant: I'll preserve that request for Dana. I haven't confirmed whether the appointment type will change.
  8. Patient: Also, please call me Alex. Alexandra is on the record, but I don't use it socially.
  9. Assistant: I'll use Alex as your preferred name while keeping the verified record linked to Alexandra.
  10. Patient: Thank you. That sorts out the name question, and I'll discuss the visit plan with Dana.
The blank box

Fictional intake form has a blank contact field; the assistant checks its meaning privately.

  1. Assistant: The emergency-contact field on your intake form is blank. Did you want help clarifying it?
  2. Patient: I wasn't sure whether it asked for my number or someone else's emergency contact.
  3. Assistant: It asks for an emergency contact. Your own telephone number is in the section above.
  4. Patient: I understand now. I'd like to discuss whom to list before giving you a name.
  5. Assistant: That's fine. I'll mark the field as not provided yet rather than assume nobody is available.
  6. Patient: Does leaving it blank mean I can't have the conversation with the clinician today?
  7. Assistant: I haven't been told that. I can ask reception about any remaining registration requirements.
  8. Patient: Please ask them, but don't enter my sister automatically just because she brought me here.
  9. Assistant: I won't infer your choice. The emergency-contact field remains open for you to clarify.
  10. Patient: Good. I can decide whom to list after I understand what the clinic needs it for.
A quieter identity check

Fictional waiting room; patient requests privacy while identifiers are checked.

  1. Patient: Could we check my details somewhere quieter? I don't want everyone hearing my birth date.
  2. Assistant: Yes, the side desk is available. We'll continue your check-in there, away from the waiting chairs.
  3. Patient: Will you need the name from my record or the name I prefer people to use?
  4. Assistant: We need patient identifiers to verify the record, plus your preferred name for conversation.
  5. Patient: My record uses Samuel, but I prefer Sam. The surname is the same on both.
  6. Assistant: Thank you, Sam. We'll keep those purposes clear rather than create a second record for the name.
  7. Patient: I thought asking for a different name might mean starting all my registration again.
  8. Assistant: We can review your name preference within the existing verified record.
  9. Patient: That's helpful. I can answer the identity questions comfortably now that we're at this desk.
  10. Assistant: Let's continue here, and then I'll confirm your check-in status without announcing your private details outside.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The schedule and the expectation
  1. Read Alex and Sam's corrected exchange. Stress annual review, recent concern, and Dana; keep the patient concern separate from the booking label.
  2. Switch roles. Repeat the privacy request and preferred-name exchange without changing the verified identity facts.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Clarify a different appointment expectation

The schedule says follow-up. Patient Morgan expected an initial consultation. Assistant Lee cannot decide suitability. Nurse Inez is available to clarify the plan.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Lee: "The recorded visit is a ___."

Answer and explanation

C. follow-up

Follow-up is the scheduled category stated in this scenario.

2. Morgan: "I expected an ___."

Answer and explanation

D. initial consultation

The patient's expectation differs from the recorded follow-up category.

3. Lee: "Nurse ___ can clarify the plan."

Answer and explanation

B. Inez

Inez is the available clinical professional named in the facts.

4. Lee: "Clinical suitability is not yet ___."

Answer and explanation

A. established

No clinical suitability decision has been supplied for this mismatch.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Another round with an AI partner

These prompts are fully written and include this lesson's facts and language. Open one, copy it as written, and paste it into a new AI chat.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 01: Clarifying the visit at check-in
Original fictional case: The schedule lists an annual review for patient Alexandra, whose verified record has already been matched through the clinic process. The patient prefers Alex and expected to discuss a recent concern. Assistant Sam cannot decide whether the appointment type is suitable. Nurse Dana is available to clarify the visit plan. No decision about the concern, billing, urgency, or an additional appointment has been supplied. Sam must acknowledge the expectation, preserve accurate identification, use Alex in conversation, and ask Dana to clarify the clinical plan.
Language workshop: The schedule and the expectation
Communication goal: Clarify a mismatch between a scheduled visit and a patient concern while respecting the preferred name.
Language guidance: An appointment label describes a scheduled category, not a clinical judgment about every concern raised that day. Do not promise that the concern will be resolved, reject it automatically, or invent a billing outcome from the schedule alone.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Thank you, Alex; I will use that name. / The schedule lists an annual review. / You expected to discuss a recent concern. / The recorded visit type and your expectation are not the same. / I cannot decide whether this appointment type meets that need. / Nurse Dana can clarify the visit plan. / We will keep the verified record linked correctly. / Let us clarify the plan before assuming how the mismatch happened. / I cannot determine charges from this appointment label alone. / I will include your stated concern in the handoff. / We can use the appropriate setting for personal details. / I cannot promise a particular clinical outcome. / Dana, can you clarify the visit plan with Alex? / The verified record says Alexandra; the preferred spoken name is Alex. / We should use the correctly matched existing record. / I will explain the mismatch to Dana so you do not have to start from nothing.
Vocabulary: check-in: Arrival process confirming a patient and the scheduled visit. / appointment type: Category describing the scheduled purpose or format of a visit. / annual review: Periodic review visit recorded as occurring yearly. / presenting concern: Issue the patient brings to the current visit. / chief complaint: Clinical-record term for the main reason for seeking care. / visit agenda: Topics planned for discussion during the encounter. / preferred name: Name a person asks others to use when addressing them. / recorded name: Name shown in the relevant patient record. / patient identifier: Information used to match a person with the correct record. / demographic details: Administrative identity and contact information in a record. / registration: Creation or updating of required administrative patient information. / encounter: A particular interaction or visit involving healthcare services. / appointment slot: Reserved time in a schedule. / scheduled purpose: Reason for a visit as recorded in the booking. / patient expectation: What a patient believes will happen during a service or visit. / clinical suitability: Whether a plan or appointment is appropriate for the clinical need. / scope of practice: Activities permitted within a role under applicable requirements. / delegation: Assignment of an activity under the applicable professional rules. / supervision: Required oversight of work by an appropriate professional. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant information to a receiving clinical professional. / visit plan: Intended approach to the current appointment. / billing category: Classification used for charging or reimbursement purposes. / identity match: Confirmation that a person corresponds to the correct record. / name pronunciation: The way a person's name is spoken.
Speaking task: Read Alex and Sam's corrected exchange. Stress annual review, recent concern, and Dana; keep the patient concern separate from the booking label. Switch roles. Repeat the privacy request and preferred-name exchange without changing the verified identity facts. Complete Morgan's four-line exchange, check the key, then read it aloud with follow-up and initial consultation clearly contrasted.
Partner: Alex: Patient / Sam: Medical assistant
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: The schedule says follow-up. Patient Morgan expected an initial consultation. Assistant Lee cannot decide suitability. Nurse Inez is available to clarify the plan.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Alex: Thank you. I do not need you to change the appointment yourself; I need someone qualified to clarify what today's visit can include.
Sam: That is the visit plan question for Dana. I will not turn the appointment label into a refusal or a guarantee before that discussion.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Language workshop: The schedule and the expectation
Communication goal: Clarify a mismatch between a scheduled visit and a patient concern while respecting the preferred name.
Language guidance: An appointment label describes a scheduled category, not a clinical judgment about every concern raised that day. Do not promise that the concern will be resolved, reject it automatically, or invent a billing outcome from the schedule alone.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Thank you, Alex; I will use that name. / The schedule lists an annual review. / You expected to discuss a recent concern. / The recorded visit type and your expectation are not the same. / I cannot decide whether this appointment type meets that need. / Nurse Dana can clarify the visit plan. / We will keep the verified record linked correctly. / Let us clarify the plan before assuming how the mismatch happened. / I cannot determine charges from this appointment label alone. / I will include your stated concern in the handoff. / We can use the appropriate setting for personal details. / I cannot promise a particular clinical outcome. / Dana, can you clarify the visit plan with Alex? / The verified record says Alexandra; the preferred spoken name is Alex. / We should use the correctly matched existing record. / I will explain the mismatch to Dana so you do not have to start from nothing.
Vocabulary: check-in: Arrival process confirming a patient and the scheduled visit. / appointment type: Category describing the scheduled purpose or format of a visit. / annual review: Periodic review visit recorded as occurring yearly. / presenting concern: Issue the patient brings to the current visit. / chief complaint: Clinical-record term for the main reason for seeking care. / visit agenda: Topics planned for discussion during the encounter. / preferred name: Name a person asks others to use when addressing them. / recorded name: Name shown in the relevant patient record. / patient identifier: Information used to match a person with the correct record. / demographic details: Administrative identity and contact information in a record. / registration: Creation or updating of required administrative patient information. / encounter: A particular interaction or visit involving healthcare services. / appointment slot: Reserved time in a schedule. / scheduled purpose: Reason for a visit as recorded in the booking. / patient expectation: What a patient believes will happen during a service or visit. / clinical suitability: Whether a plan or appointment is appropriate for the clinical need. / scope of practice: Activities permitted within a role under applicable requirements. / delegation: Assignment of an activity under the applicable professional rules. / supervision: Required oversight of work by an appropriate professional. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant information to a receiving clinical professional. / visit plan: Intended approach to the current appointment. / billing category: Classification used for charging or reimbursement purposes. / identity match: Confirmation that a person corresponds to the correct record. / name pronunciation: The way a person's name is spoken.
Speaking task: Read Alex and Sam's corrected exchange. Stress annual review, recent concern, and Dana; keep the patient concern separate from the booking label. Switch roles. Repeat the privacy request and preferred-name exchange without changing the verified identity facts. Complete Morgan's four-line exchange, check the key, then read it aloud with follow-up and initial consultation clearly contrasted.
Partner: Alex: Patient / Sam: Medical assistant
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: The schedule says follow-up. Patient Morgan expected an initial consultation. Assistant Lee cannot decide suitability. Nurse Inez is available to clarify the plan.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Alex: Thank you. I do not need you to change the appointment yourself; I need someone qualified to clarify what today's visit can include.
Sam: That is the visit plan question for Dana. I will not turn the appointment label into a refusal or a guarantee before that discussion.
Alex: All right. Can you let Dana know I am here? I would like to settle the visit plan before I explain everything again.
Sam: Correct. I will record the appointment type accurately and pass on your expectation. We will keep the two facts clear while Dana clarifies the next step.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Course: Medical Assisting English
Professional audience: Medical assistants and outpatient clinic support staff
Scope: All patients, records, times, and cases are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not clinical assessment, triage, prescribing, specimen procedures, legal advice, or professional certification. Duties depend on local law, training, delegation, supervision, and clinic policy. Follow actual clinical and emergency procedures; these dialogues do not establish that any symptom can safely wait. US sources provide context, not universal rules. Use actual dates and required identifiers in real records.

02Rooming and factual patient reportsReport, observation, assessment+

Your goal

Give a concise patient report that separates the patient words, observed behavior, and unassessed clinical questions.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

Report, observation, assessment

During rooming, patient Pat says, "I have felt tired since Monday." Assistant Rosa observed Pat answering questions while seated. Rosa has not assessed the cause or severity, and no measurement values are supplied. Nurse Dana is receiving the report. The conversation must preserve the onset as reported by Pat, state the limited observation, and avoid filling missing fields with normal values. Sitting and answering questions do not establish that the concern is mild or safe to defer. Actual urgency must follow the clinic clinical process.

Rosa: Medical assistant / Dana: Receiving nurse

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What onset did Pat report?

Answer and explanation

C. Monday

Monday is the onset reported by Pat, not an independently measured event.

2. What did Rosa observe?

Answer and explanation

B. Pat answering questions while seated

Only the seated question-answering observation is supplied; measurements and conclusions are absent.

3. What should remain unassessed in the report?

Answer and explanation

A. Cause and severity

Rosa has not assessed cause or severity and must not manufacture those findings.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Not reported does not mean absent, and not measured does not mean normal. A pertinent negative requires a relevant question or assessment. Do not populate a record from expectation or convert an empty field into reassurance.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
rooming
Preparing a patient for the clinical encounter under the clinic process.complete the rooming process
patient-reported symptom
Experience described by the patient rather than independently observed.attribute a patient-reported symptom
onset
When a symptom or condition began.clarify the reported onset
duration
Length of time an experience or condition has continued.document symptom duration
severity
Degree or intensity of a condition or symptom.assess severity
observation
Something directly noticed and described without inventing its cause.record an observation
subjective information
Information based on a person's reported experience.attribute subjective information
objective finding
Observed or measured finding, with its source and context.document an objective finding
baseline
Usual or earlier state used for comparison.establish the baseline
change from baseline
Difference from a known usual or previous state.report a change from baseline
associated symptom
Additional symptom occurring with the main concern.ask about associated symptoms
functional impact
Effect on a person's ability to perform ordinary activities.describe functional impact
vital signs
Physiological measurements such as pulse and temperature.record measured vital signs
blood pressure
Pressure exerted by blood within the circulation, measured with relevant units.document blood pressure
pulse rate
Number of palpable arterial pulses per unit of time.measure pulse rate
respiratory rate
Number of breaths per unit of time.record respiratory rate
oxygen saturation
Percentage of hemoglobin oxygen-binding sites occupied by oxygen, estimated or measured by an appropriate method.report oxygen saturation
temperature
Measure of how hot or cold the body or another object is.record the measured temperature
unit of measurement
Standard used to express a measured quantity.include the unit of measurement
measurement time
Time at which a measurement was actually taken.preserve the measurement time
clinical assessment
Qualified evaluation of health information and the patient's condition.request clinical assessment
unassessed
Not evaluated through the relevant assessment.mark severity as unassessed
pertinent negative
Relevant absence established by asking or assessing, not by silence.document a pertinent negative
source attribution
Identification of where a statement or finding came from.preserve source attribution

Pat answering questions while seated is a limited observation. It does not establish alertness on a formal scale, normal vital signs, a cause of tiredness, or a severity rating. Preserve what was actually observed and leave interpretation with the appropriate clinician.

C Notice the language

Neutral is not dismissive

A factual report can be both concise and attentive. Use the patient words and name the limits of the observation. Avoid vague labels such as fine or difficult, which replace useful information with an impression and can distort the clinical handoff.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Attribute the words
Pat reports feeling tired since Monday.
Quote accurately
Pat said, "I have felt tired since Monday."
Describe the observation
I observed Pat answering questions while seated.
Name the limit
I have not assessed the cause or severity.
Do not invent values
No measurement values are available in this report.
Separate sources
That is the patient report, not a measured finding.
Keep onset attributed
Monday is the onset Pat reported.
Avoid a vague reassurance
I cannot describe the concern as mild from that observation.
State what is missing
The relevant clinical assessment is still needed.
Do not imply absence
An unreported symptom is not automatically absent.
Keep the handoff concise
I will give the report, observation, and outstanding assessment separately.
Use units accurately
Any actual measurement needs its correct value, unit, and context.
Refer the judgment
Dana, please assess the clinical concern through our process.
Preserve uncertainty
I do not have a verified explanation for the tiredness.
Confirm receipt
Have you received Pat's report and the limits of my observation?
Close factually
The cause and severity remain unassessed in my report.
6 language and precision notes
Pat reports
Keeps the patient's experience distinct from an assistant's clinical conclusion.
I observed
Identifies a firsthand observation and its source.
Since Monday
Gives the reported starting point without inventing a date or duration.
Have not assessed
Describes the actual evidence limit, not a negative result.
Not automatically absent
Prevents a blank field from becoming a clinical finding.
Separately
Helps a receiving professional distinguish evidence types quickly.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which report is supported?

Answer and explanation

B. Pat reports tiredness since Monday; I observed Pat answering questions while seated.

The supported report preserves both supplied sources without adding a cause, severity, or safety judgment.

2. What belongs in the temperature field when no measurement is available?

Answer and explanation

C. No invented numeric value

No measurement is supplied; neither a previous value nor an impression can establish today's temperature.

3. Why is "Pat is fine" inadequate?

Answer and explanation

C. It substitutes a broad conclusion for the limited observation.

Fine does not describe the actual observation and implies a clinical reassurance that Rosa has not established.

4. Which statement about other symptoms is accurate?

Answer and explanation

B. No other symptoms are supplied; that does not prove their absence.

Silence in the case does not constitute a negative clinical history or assessment.

E Conversations

Report, observation, assessment

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Rosa: Medical assistant / Dana: Receiving nurse

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • baseline
  • onset
  • patient-reported symptom
  • Clinical assessment
  • pertinent negative
  • observation
  • severity
  • source attribution
  • unassessed
  • Vital signs
  1. Rosa
    Dana, I have Pat's rooming report. Pat said, "I have felt tired since Monday." I want to keep that separate from what I observed.
  2. Dana
    Please give the 1 clearly. What came from Pat, what did you directly observe, and what has not been assessed?
  3. Rosa
    The tiredness and Monday starting point came from Pat. I observed Pat answering questions while seated. I have not assessed the cause or severity.
  4. Dana
    Good. The reported 2 is Monday. Do not replace it with a guessed date or calculate a duration from an assumed calendar.
  5. Rosa
    The handoff draft says looks fine. I will replace that with what I saw: Pat was seated and answering my questions.
  6. Dana
    Exactly. Keep the 3 specific. Answering questions while seated does not establish the severity of tiredness or whether a concern can safely wait.
  7. Rosa
    I also need to flag the blank measurement fields. I have no values to report; those fields should not be read as normal findings.
  8. Dana
    Correct. 4 need actual values and appropriate context. A missing measurement cannot be converted into normal by choosing a familiar number.
  9. Rosa
    Should I write that Pat denied other symptoms? None are included in the information I am handing over, but I have not established their absence.
  10. Dana
    No. A 5 must come from the relevant question or assessment. Lack of a statement in this report is not a denial.
  11. Rosa
    I will keep the original wording in quotation marks when I use it exactly. If I summarize, I will still identify Pat as the source.
  12. Dana
    That preserves the 6. Do not translate tired into a specific diagnosis or imply that Pat used a technical term they did not actually say.
  13. Rosa
    I also cannot say this is a change from Pat's normal condition unless I have an established usual state to compare it with.
  14. Dana
    Right. A 7 is not something we should invent. Your report can remain useful without adding a comparison that has not been obtained.
  15. Rosa
    The handoff can be short: Pat reports tiredness since Monday; observed answering questions while seated; cause and severity not assessed by me.
  16. Dana
    That clearly identifies what is 8. It does not minimize the concern, and it leaves the clinical questions visible for the proper assessment.
  17. Rosa
    Have you received those points? I do not want the phrase answering questions to be passed on as a formal severity rating later.
  18. Dana
    I have received the report and its limits. 9 is separate, and the concern will follow our clinical process rather than an administrative assumption.
  19. Rosa
    I will record the report accordingly and preserve what I actually observed. I will not add a measurement, diagnosis, or assurance about waiting.
  20. Dana
    Thank you. Keep the 10 question open in that record. Clear limits help the receiving team know what still requires attention.

Answers and explanations
  1. source attribution Turn 2. Source attribution identifies Pat as the source of the symptom report and Rosa as the source of the observation.
  2. onset Turn 4. Onset is the stated beginning of the symptom; Monday is attributed to Pat rather than independently established.
  3. observation Turn 6. The observation is Pat answering questions while seated; it does not establish a diagnosis, severity, or normal measurements.
  4. Vital signs Turn 8. Vital signs require actual measurements and context; expected or typical values cannot fill missing clinical data.
  5. pertinent negative Turn 10. A pertinent negative is an established relevant absence, not a conclusion drawn from an unmentioned symptom.
  6. patient-reported symptom Turn 12. The patient-reported symptom is the experienced tiredness described by Pat; attribution remains necessary even when it is paraphrased.
  7. baseline Turn 14. A baseline supplies the known usual or earlier state needed for a meaningful comparison; it is not supplied here.
  8. unassessed Turn 16. Unassessed means not evaluated through the relevant process, not evaluated and found normal or harmless.
  9. Clinical assessment Turn 18. Clinical assessment is the qualified evaluation still needed; it must not be inferred from Rosa's limited rooming observation.
  10. severity Turn 20. Severity remains unassessed in Rosa's report and cannot be inferred merely from Pat speaking while seated.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Rosa: Dana, I have Pat's rooming report. Pat said, "I have felt tired since Monday." I want to keep that separate from what I observed.
  2. Dana: Please give the source attribution clearly. What came from Pat, what did you directly observe, and what has not been assessed?
  3. Rosa: The tiredness and Monday starting point came from Pat. I observed Pat answering questions while seated. I have not assessed the cause or severity.
  4. Dana: Good. The reported onset is Monday. Do not replace it with a guessed date or calculate a duration from an assumed calendar.
  5. Rosa: The handoff draft says looks fine. I will replace that with what I saw: Pat was seated and answering my questions.
  6. Dana: Exactly. Keep the observation specific. Answering questions while seated does not establish the severity of tiredness or whether a concern can safely wait.
  7. Rosa: I also need to flag the blank measurement fields. I have no values to report; those fields should not be read as normal findings.
  8. Dana: Correct. Vital signs need actual values and appropriate context. A missing measurement cannot be converted into normal by choosing a familiar number.
  9. Rosa: Should I write that Pat denied other symptoms? None are included in the information I am handing over, but I have not established their absence.
  10. Dana: No. A pertinent negative must come from the relevant question or assessment. Lack of a statement in this report is not a denial.
  11. Rosa: I will keep the original wording in quotation marks when I use it exactly. If I summarize, I will still identify Pat as the source.
  12. Dana: That preserves the patient-reported symptom. Do not translate tired into a specific diagnosis or imply that Pat used a technical term they did not actually say.
  13. Rosa: I also cannot say this is a change from Pat's normal condition unless I have an established usual state to compare it with.
  14. Dana: Right. A baseline is not something we should invent. Your report can remain useful without adding a comparison that has not been obtained.
  15. Rosa: The handoff can be short: Pat reports tiredness since Monday; observed answering questions while seated; cause and severity not assessed by me.
  16. Dana: That clearly identifies what is unassessed. It does not minimize the concern, and it leaves the clinical questions visible for the proper assessment.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

What Pat reported

Fictional staff handoff using the lesson case, without clinical assessment.

  1. Assistant: Pat reports tiredness since Monday and wants to discuss that change with the clinician.
  2. Nurse: Which part did Pat tell you, and what did you observe yourself during rooming?
  3. Assistant: The reported symptom and onset came from Pat. I observed Pat answering questions while seated.
  4. Nurse: Have you established a cause, or is the reason for that change still unassessed?
  5. Assistant: I haven't established a cause or severity. I don't want my observation to imply either conclusion.
  6. Nurse: Did Pat describe it as constant, or have you only confirmed when it began?
  7. Assistant: Only the reported start on Monday. The pattern wasn't clarified in our conversation.
  8. Nurse: I'll take that distinction into the assessment. Please keep the missing detail explicit in your handoff.
  9. Assistant: Then the handoff is reported tiredness since Monday, answers while seated observed, pattern not clarified.
  10. Nurse: That's clear. I'll ask Pat about the unresolved details rather than infer them from the note.
Yesterday or last evening

Fictional patient clarifies the time meant by yesterday; no diagnostic conclusion.

  1. Assistant: You said the change began yesterday. Do you mean morning, afternoon, or evening?
  2. Patient: I meant after dinner last evening. I wasn't trying to say it lasted all day.
  3. Assistant: Thank you. I'll pass on after dinner yesterday evening rather than yesterday morning.
  4. Patient: I can't remember the exact hour. Is it a problem if I don't give a precise time?
  5. Assistant: You can say you don't know the exact time. I shouldn't supply one for you.
  6. Patient: Could you also tell the clinician it happened again this morning, not continuously overnight?
  7. Assistant: Yes, I'll preserve that distinction: last evening and again this morning, with the exact times uncertain.
  8. Patient: I don't know why it happened. Please don't write that I blamed the food.
  9. Assistant: I haven't added a cause. Your account describes timing, and the clinician can explore its meaning.
  10. Patient: That's what I wanted. I was using dinner to describe the time, not an explanation.
A measurement entry without its time

Fictional chart handoff; a vital-sign entry lacks a timestamp and its origin is unclear.

  1. Assistant: The vital signs entry lacks a timestamp. I can't tell whether it belongs to today's visit.
  2. Nurse: Did you take that measurement, or are you reviewing an entry already in the chart?
  3. Assistant: I'm reviewing an existing entry. I haven't recorded it as a measurement I obtained today.
  4. Nurse: Then please don't call it the current reading until we identify its source and time.
  5. Assistant: Could you review the entry while I explain the uncertainty to the rooming colleague?
  6. Nurse: Yes. I'll check the record history and determine what needs clarification for the visit.
  7. Assistant: The colleague asked whether the value means anything clinically. I haven't interpreted it.
  8. Nurse: Keep that question with the clinician. Our immediate documentation issue is when and where it originated.
  9. Assistant: I'll hand it over as an untimed existing entry, not a new observation from rooming.
  10. Nurse: That wording lets us resolve the source without turning an unknown timestamp into a clinical conclusion.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Report, observation, assessment
  1. Read Rosa and Dana's corrected report. Contrast Pat reports with I observed; keep the reported onset attached to Pat.
  2. Switch roles. Repeat the unmeasured-vitals and pertinent-negative exchanges without adding a normal finding.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Attribute a different symptom report

Patient Lee reports dizziness since breakfast. Assistant Noor observed Lee speaking while seated. No measurements or cause assessment are supplied. Nurse Inez receives the report.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Noor: "Lee reports ___ since breakfast."

Answer and explanation

B. dizziness

Dizziness is the symptom attributed to Lee in the facts.

2. Inez: "The reported onset is ___."

Answer and explanation

A. breakfast

Breakfast is the reported starting point, not a measured cause.

3. Noor: "I observed Lee speaking while ___."

Answer and explanation

C. seated

The supplied observation concerns speaking in a seated position.

4. Inez: "The cause remains ___."

Answer and explanation

A. unassessed

No assessment of the cause has been supplied here.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary

Original fictional case: During rooming, patient Pat says, "I have felt tired since Monday." Assistant Rosa observed Pat answering questions while seated. Rosa has not assessed the cause or severity, and no measurement values are supplied. Nurse Dana is receiving the report. The conversation must preserve the onset as reported by Pat, state the limited observation, and avoid filling missing fields with normal values. Sitting and answering questions do not establish that the concern is mild or safe to defer. Actual urgency must follow the clinic clinical process.
Language workshop: Report, observation, assessment
Communication goal: Give a concise patient report that separates the patient words, observed behavior, and unassessed clinical questions.
Language guidance: Not reported does not mean absent, and not measured does not mean normal. A pertinent negative requires a relevant question or assessment. Do not populate a record from expectation or convert an empty field into reassurance.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Pat reports feeling tired since Monday. / Pat said, "I have felt tired since Monday." / I observed Pat answering questions while seated. / I have not assessed the cause or severity. / No measurement values are available in this report. / That is the patient report, not a measured finding. / Monday is the onset Pat reported. / I cannot describe the concern as mild from that observation. / The relevant clinical assessment is still needed. / An unreported symptom is not automatically absent. / I will give the report, observation, and outstanding assessment separately. / Any actual measurement needs its correct value, unit, and context. / Dana, please assess the clinical concern through our process. / I do not have a verified explanation for the tiredness. / Have you received Pat's report and the limits of my observation? / The cause and severity remain unassessed in my report.
Vocabulary: rooming: Preparing a patient for the clinical encounter under the clinic process. / patient-reported symptom: Experience described by the patient rather than independently observed. / onset: When a symptom or condition began. / duration: Length of time an experience or condition has continued. / severity: Degree or intensity of a condition or symptom. / observation: Something directly noticed and described without inventing its cause. / subjective information: Information based on a person's reported experience. / objective finding: Observed or measured finding, with its source and context. / baseline: Usual or earlier state used for comparison. / change from baseline: Difference from a known usual or previous state. / associated symptom: Additional symptom occurring with the main concern. / functional impact: Effect on a person's ability to perform ordinary activities. / vital signs: Physiological measurements such as pulse and temperature. / blood pressure: Pressure exerted by blood within the circulation, measured with relevant units. / pulse rate: Number of palpable arterial pulses per unit of time. / respiratory rate: Number of breaths per unit of time. / oxygen saturation: Percentage of hemoglobin oxygen-binding sites occupied by oxygen, estimated or measured by an appropriate method. / temperature: Measure of how hot or cold the body or another object is. / unit of measurement: Standard used to express a measured quantity. / measurement time: Time at which a measurement was actually taken. / clinical assessment: Qualified evaluation of health information and the patient's condition. / unassessed: Not evaluated through the relevant assessment. / pertinent negative: Relevant absence established by asking or assessing, not by silence. / source attribution: Identification of where a statement or finding came from.
Speaking task: Read Rosa and Dana's corrected report. Contrast Pat reports with I observed; keep the reported onset attached to Pat. Switch roles. Rosa: I will record the report accordingly and preserve what I actually observed. I will not add a measurement, diagnosis, or assurance about waiting.
Dana: Thank you. Keep the severity question open in that record. Clear limits help the receiving team know what still requires attention.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
