03Medication histories with clear sourcesTwo sources, one unresolved entry+

Your goal

Document a reported medication change without treating an unverified history as a new clinical instruction.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

Two sources, one unresolved entry

Patient Ren reports that another clinic stopped one medicine. The local medication list still includes it. Ren does not have the outside instruction available. Assistant Talia cannot verify discontinuation, change the list as a clinical decision, or tell Ren to start, stop, or restart treatment. Nurse Dana can review the discrepancy with the clinician. The immediate communication task is to record the patient report and the conflicting local entry with clear sources, then connect the question with Dana. No medicine name, dose, or timing decision is supplied.

Ren: Patient / Talia: Medical assistant

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does Ren report?

Answer and explanation

D. Another clinic stopped one medicine.

Ren reports an outside change, but the instruction is not available for verification.

2. What conflicts with that report?

Answer and explanation

A. The medicine remains on the local list.

The local list still includes the medicine, creating the stated discrepancy.

3. What can Dana do?

Answer and explanation

D. Review the discrepancy with the clinician

Dana is the identified route for appropriate review, not a supplied treatment decision.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

An active entry can reflect the state of a record rather than a verified current instruction. Conversely, a patient report deserves documentation but does not automatically complete reconciliation. Preserve both until the authorized review resolves the discrepancy.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
medication history
Account of medicines used or reported, with relevant sources and timing.obtain a medication history
medication reconciliation
Process of comparing medication information and resolving discrepancies appropriately.support medication reconciliation
local medication list
Medicine record held by the current organization.review the local medication list
outside instruction
Direction originating from another healthcare source.verify the outside instruction
discontinuation
Ending a medicine or order through the relevant clinical process.confirm discontinuation
patient report
Information supplied by the patient about their experience or history.document the patient report
active entry
Record item currently marked active in a system.verify an active entry
historical entry
Record of earlier information rather than necessarily current treatment.distinguish a historical entry
duplicate entry
Repeated record item that may need clarification.investigate a duplicate entry
omission
Information missing from a list or account.identify a possible omission
discrepancy
Difference between sources requiring clarification.flag a medication discrepancy
prescribing clinician
Professional authorized to prescribe the relevant medicine.contact the prescribing clinician
dose
Amount intended to be taken or administered at one time.verify the prescribed dose
strength
Amount of active ingredient in a specified unit of product.distinguish strength from dose
dosage form
Physical form of a medicine, such as tablet or solution.confirm the dosage form
route
Way a medicine is taken or administered.verify the route
frequency
How often a medicine is taken or administered.clarify the frequency
as-needed instruction
Direction allowing use under specified conditions rather than a fixed schedule alone.verify the as-needed instruction
over-the-counter medicine
Medicine available without a prescription under applicable rules.record over-the-counter medicines
dietary supplement
Product used to supplement dietary intake, subject to relevant regulation.ask about dietary supplements
allergy history
Account of suspected or confirmed allergic reactions with relevant details.document allergy history
adverse reaction
Harmful or unwanted response associated with a medicine or other exposure.report an adverse reaction
adherence
Extent to which medicine use corresponds with the agreed plan.discuss adherence without blame
verification source
Person, document, or system used to confirm information.identify the verification source

A history question is not permission to advise starting, stopping, or restarting a medicine. Dose, strength, route, and frequency describe different aspects of an instruction. Do not fill any of them from memory or from a similar product.

C Notice the language

A discrepancy is not a credibility contest

Do not make the patient defend their memory against the computer. Acknowledge the report and preserve both sources for review. Respect does not require treating an unverified instruction as confirmed, and careful verification does not require dismissing the patient.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the report
You report that another clinic stopped this medicine.
State the local source
The local medication list still includes it.
Name the discrepancy
Those two sources need to be reconciled.
Avoid judging memory
I will record what you remember and ask the clinical team to check the difference.
Keep attribution
I will record that this information came from you.
Identify the missing evidence
The outside instruction is not available here.
Give the review route
Dana can review the discrepancy with the clinician.
Avoid treatment advice
I cannot tell you to start, stop, or restart it.
Do not silently delete
We need the appropriate review before treating the entry as resolved.
Preserve the details
I will keep the original source and the reported change clear.
Separate strength and dose
The product strength and prescribed amount are different details.
Include nonprescription use
Relevant nonprescription medicines and supplements also belong in an accurate history.
Do not invent a reaction
No allergy or adverse reaction is established by this discrepancy.
Keep urgency separate
A current medicine concern needs the appropriate clinical route.
Confirm the handoff
Dana will receive both the patient report and the local-list difference.
Close without a new instruction
This conversation records the question; it does not change your treatment.
6 language and precision notes
You report
Attributes the change without dismissing it or confirming it prematurely.
Still includes
Describes the local record rather than asserting that treatment must continue.
Those two sources
Treats the problem as reconciliation instead of a contest over credibility.
Not available here
Names the evidence gap without claiming the outside instruction never existed.
Start, stop, or restart
Makes the clinical boundary explicit in ordinary language.
Does not change
Prevents a history discussion from being mistaken for prescribing advice.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which note is accurate?

Answer and explanation

D. Patient reports outside discontinuation; local list still includes medicine; instruction unavailable; referred to Dana.

The note preserves the two sources, missing instruction, and review route without inventing a treatment decision.

2. What does the active local entry establish by itself?

Answer and explanation

D. The medicine remains listed locally, not that a new instruction has been verified.

A record status does not automatically resolve a conflicting report or create a new prescription.

3. Which reply crosses Talia's stated role?

Answer and explanation

B. Restart it until we hear otherwise.

Restarting is a treatment instruction that Talia is not authorized to supply in this case.

4. Which additional conclusion is unsupported?

Answer and explanation

D. The discrepancy proves an allergy.

No reaction or allergy details are supplied; a record discrepancy cannot establish either.

Check explanations in the book, page 89 ↗

E Conversations

Two sources, one unresolved entry

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Ren: Patient / Talia: Medical assistant

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • adherence
  • verification source
  • local medication list
  • Strength
  • Medication reconciliation
  • outside instruction
  • active entry
  • discontinuation
  • discrepancy
  • patient report
  1. Ren
    Your list still shows a medicine that another clinic told me to stop. I do not have their instruction with me, but I remember the conversation.
  2. Talia
    Thank you for telling me. I will record your 1 and the difference in our list. I am not going to dismiss it because the computer still shows an entry.
  3. Ren
    Does the computer mean I should be taking it after all? I do not want two clinics giving me opposite directions without explaining why.
  4. Talia
    The 2 is a record we need to review. It does not let me give you a new instruction to take, stop, or restart a medicine.
  5. Ren
    Then please do not remove it and say everything is sorted either. I want the right person to check what the other clinic actually intended.
  6. Talia
    Exactly. We need the 3 and appropriate review. Nurse Dana can take the discrepancy to the clinician; I cannot verify the change myself from this conversation.
  7. Ren
    I appreciate that. Sometimes questions about medicines sound as though I am being accused of not following instructions, even when I am trying to explain a change.
  8. Talia
    I will not label this an 4 problem without evidence. The immediate issue is that your report and our entry differ, and we need to clarify the plan.
  9. Ren
    I do not have the other clinic's instructions with me. Can you help get them checked? I am worried about being given conflicting advice again.
  10. Talia
    Yes. The 5 is still to be established through the review. I will distinguish your report from a verified clinical decision.
  11. Ren
    The medicine being marked active is what confused me. Is that label always current, or can the record need updating after another clinic makes a change?
  12. Talia
    An 6 tells us how the item is marked. It does not settle this conflicting information, so we must not treat that label as the answer.
  13. Ren
    I also do not have the exact strength or dose in front of me. I would rather say that than give you a number from memory.
  14. Talia
    That is helpful. 7 and dose are different details, and neither should be guessed. We will preserve what is known and identify what requires verification.
  15. Ren
    Can Dana speak with me about it? The list worries me because I still need to know which instruction I should follow.
  16. Talia
    It is a 8 needing the appropriate review. I will pass on both sources and the missing instruction rather than silently choose one over the other.
  17. Ren
    Please make sure any current question about what I should do reaches the clinical team. I do not want the record discussion mistaken for actual advice.
  18. Talia
    I will route that clinical question appropriately. 9 and clinical guidance need the proper owner, and recording a history does not authorize me to change treatment.
  19. Ren
    Then I understand the position: I reported an outside change, your list still includes the medicine, and Dana will review the difference with the clinician.
  20. Talia
    Correct. The reported 10 is not yet verified here. I will keep the source clear, preserve the question, and connect the review with Dana.

Answers and explanations
  1. patient report Turn 2. The patient report attributes the claimed outside change to Ren without pretending the original instruction has been verified.
  2. local medication list Turn 4. The local medication list is the current organization's record, not automatic proof of a verified present treatment instruction.
  3. outside instruction Turn 6. The outside instruction is the missing source needed for appropriate verification; its absence does not prove Ren's report is false.
  4. adherence Turn 8. Adherence concerns use in relation to an agreed plan; a conflict between records does not establish deliberate nonadherence.
  5. verification source Turn 10. A verification source is the document or professional used to confirm information; Ren's attributed report is not a substitute for that review.
  6. active entry Turn 12. An active entry describes the system status and may require reconciliation; it cannot independently settle the reported outside change.
  7. Strength Turn 14. Strength describes active ingredient per product unit and is different from the amount prescribed for a dose.
  8. discrepancy Turn 16. A discrepancy is the unresolved difference between Ren's report and the local record, not a confirmed treatment error or new instruction.
  9. Medication reconciliation Turn 18. Medication reconciliation compares and resolves medication information through the authorized process; merely documenting a conflict does not complete it.
  10. discontinuation Turn 20. Discontinuation is the claimed stopping of the medicine; it remains a reported change rather than a verified decision in this conversation.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Ren: Your list still shows a medicine that another clinic told me to stop. I do not have their instruction with me, but I remember the conversation.
  2. Talia: Thank you for telling me. I will record your patient report and the difference in our list. I am not going to dismiss it because the computer still shows an entry.
  3. Ren: Does the computer mean I should be taking it after all? I do not want two clinics giving me opposite directions without explaining why.
  4. Talia: The local medication list is a record we need to review. It does not let me give you a new instruction to take, stop, or restart a medicine.
  5. Ren: Then please do not remove it and say everything is sorted either. I want the right person to check what the other clinic actually intended.
  6. Talia: Exactly. We need the outside instruction and appropriate review. Nurse Dana can take the discrepancy to the clinician; I cannot verify the change myself from this conversation.
  7. Ren: I appreciate that. Sometimes questions about medicines sound as though I am being accused of not following instructions, even when I am trying to explain a change.
  8. Talia: I will not label this an adherence problem without evidence. The immediate issue is that your report and our entry differ, and we need to clarify the plan.
  9. Ren: I do not have the other clinic's instructions with me. Can you help get them checked? I am worried about being given conflicting advice again.
  10. Talia: Yes. The verification source is still to be established through the review. I will distinguish your report from a verified clinical decision.
  11. Ren: The medicine being marked active is what confused me. Is that label always current, or can the record need updating after another clinic makes a change?
  12. Talia: An active entry tells us how the item is marked. It does not settle this conflicting information, so we must not treat that label as the answer.
  13. Ren: I also do not have the exact strength or dose in front of me. I would rather say that than give you a number from memory.
  14. Talia: That is helpful. Strength and dose are different details, and neither should be guessed. We will preserve what is known and identify what requires verification.
  15. Ren: Can Dana speak with me about it? The list worries me because I still need to know which instruction I should follow.
  16. Talia: It is a discrepancy needing the appropriate review. I will pass on both sources and the missing instruction rather than silently choose one over the other.
  17. Ren: Please make sure any current question about what I should do reaches the clinical team. I do not want the record discussion mistaken for actual advice.
  18. Talia: I will route that clinical question appropriately. Medication reconciliation and clinical guidance need the proper owner, and recording a history does not authorize me to change treatment.
  19. Ren: Then I understand the position: I reported an outside change, your list still includes the medicine, and Dana will review the difference with the clinician.
  20. Talia: Correct. The reported discontinuation is not yet verified here. I will keep the source clear, preserve the question, and connect the review with Dana.
Dialogue in the book, page 28 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A reported change from another clinic

Fictional medication-history exchange using the lesson case.

  1. Patient: The other clinic stopped one medicine, but it's still on the list you gave me.
  2. Assistant: I'll request medication reconciliation with the clinician. Do you have the other clinic's instruction?
  3. Patient: No, I remember the discussion, but I don't have a letter or updated list.
  4. Assistant: Then I'll attribute the change to your report and explain that confirmation is still missing.
  5. Patient: Can you simply take it off so we don't have to discuss it again?
  6. Assistant: The clinician needs to reconcile the difference. I can't settle the current medication plan myself.
  7. Patient: Please tell Dana I want the two lists compared, not another printed copy of this one.
  8. Assistant: I will. The request is to review the conflicting information and clarify the current list with you.
  9. Patient: Should I change what I take tonight while this is checked?
  10. Assistant: That's a medication question for the clinician. I'll include it explicitly in the handoff to Dana.
A supplement and an uncertain name

Fictional history conversation; the patient has an unlabeled memory, not a verified product name.

  1. Assistant: Are there other products you want the clinician to know about, including supplements or nonprescription medicines?
  2. Patient: I take a supplement, but I remember the package color better than the actual name.
  3. Assistant: I won't identify it from color. Can you provide the packaging name later?
  4. Patient: Yes, my partner can bring the package. I hadn't realized the name mattered to this conversation.
  5. Assistant: It helps the clinician review what you report. For now, I'll note that the name is unconfirmed.
  6. Patient: Please don't choose the product that sounds most similar from the list on your screen.
  7. Assistant: I won't. I'll say supplement reported, product name not yet provided, and packaging to follow.
  8. Patient: Could you ask the clinician whether they want to discuss it before I leave?
  9. Assistant: Yes, I'll include your question. I can't assess the supplement or recommend changes.
  10. Patient: I'll ask my partner to bring the packaging so the clinician has the actual name.
An allergy entry needs a source

Fictional chart query: blank allergy field and patient report require clinician review.

  1. Assistant: The allergy-history field is blank, but the patient reports a previous reaction they want to discuss.
  2. Nurse: A blank field doesn't establish no allergies. What did the patient actually tell you?
  3. Assistant: They remember a reaction but are unsure of the medicine name and the date.
  4. Nurse: Please preserve that uncertainty and bring the question to me for clinical review.
  5. Assistant: Should I call it a confirmed allergy before you've spoken with the patient?
  6. Nurse: No. Attribute it to the patient, and don't turn the blank field into a negative finding either.
  7. Assistant: I'll say reaction reported, medicine and date uncertain, allergy history awaiting clinician clarification.
  8. Nurse: That's specific. Have you offered any explanation of what the reaction means or how to manage it?
  9. Assistant: No. I told the patient you would discuss it with them, without interpreting the experience.
  10. Nurse: Thank you. I'll take the history question now and address the unresolved details directly.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Two sources, one unresolved entry
  1. Read Ren's question and Talia's responses using the checked answers. Stress reports, active, unavailable, and review.
  2. Switch roles. Repeat the exchange about the outside instruction without adding a dose or restart recommendation.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Report a different medication-list change

Patient Ellis reports an outside dose change. The local record is unchanged, and the outside instruction is unavailable. Nurse Priya accepts the query. No new dose is supplied.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Assistant: "Ellis reports an outside ___ change."

Answer and explanation

C. dose

The patient reports a dose change, not an established allergy.

2. Nurse: "The local record remains ___."

Answer and explanation

A. unchanged

The local record has not changed in the supplied facts.

3. Assistant: "The outside instruction is ___."

Answer and explanation

A. unavailable

The outside instruction is not available for verification here.

4. Nurse: "The receiving owner is ___."

Answer and explanation

B. Priya

Priya has explicitly accepted the query for appropriate review.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 03: Medication histories with clear sources
Original fictional case: Patient Ren reports that another clinic stopped one medicine. The local medication list still includes it. Ren does not have the outside instruction available. Assistant Talia cannot verify discontinuation, change the list as a clinical decision, or tell Ren to start, stop, or restart treatment. Nurse Dana can review the discrepancy with the clinician. The immediate communication task is to record the patient report and the conflicting local entry with clear sources, then connect the question with Dana. No medicine name, dose, or timing decision is supplied.
Language workshop: Two sources, one unresolved entry
Communication goal: Document a reported medication change without treating an unverified history as a new clinical instruction.
Language guidance: An active entry can reflect the state of a record rather than a verified current instruction. Conversely, a patient report deserves documentation but does not automatically complete reconciliation. Preserve both until the authorized review resolves the discrepancy.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): You report that another clinic stopped this medicine. / The local medication list still includes it. / Those two sources need to be reconciled. / I will record what you remember and ask the clinical team to check the difference. / I will record that this information came from you. / The outside instruction is not available here. / Dana can review the discrepancy with the clinician. / I cannot tell you to start, stop, or restart it. / We need the appropriate review before treating the entry as resolved. / I will keep the original source and the reported change clear. / The product strength and prescribed amount are different details. / Relevant nonprescription medicines and supplements also belong in an accurate history. / No allergy or adverse reaction is established by this discrepancy. / A current medicine concern needs the appropriate clinical route. / Dana will receive both the patient report and the local-list difference. / This conversation records the question; it does not change your treatment.
Vocabulary: medication history: Account of medicines used or reported, with relevant sources and timing. / medication reconciliation: Process of comparing medication information and resolving discrepancies appropriately. / local medication list: Medicine record held by the current organization. / outside instruction: Direction originating from another healthcare source. / discontinuation: Ending a medicine or order through the relevant clinical process. / patient report: Information supplied by the patient about their experience or history. / active entry: Record item currently marked active in a system. / historical entry: Record of earlier information rather than necessarily current treatment. / duplicate entry: Repeated record item that may need clarification. / omission: Information missing from a list or account. / discrepancy: Difference between sources requiring clarification. / prescribing clinician: Professional authorized to prescribe the relevant medicine. / dose: Amount intended to be taken or administered at one time. / strength: Amount of active ingredient in a specified unit of product. / dosage form: Physical form of a medicine, such as tablet or solution. / route: Way a medicine is taken or administered. / frequency: How often a medicine is taken or administered. / as-needed instruction: Direction allowing use under specified conditions rather than a fixed schedule alone. / over-the-counter medicine: Medicine available without a prescription under applicable rules. / dietary supplement: Product used to supplement dietary intake, subject to relevant regulation. / allergy history: Account of suspected or confirmed allergic reactions with relevant details. / adverse reaction: Harmful or unwanted response associated with a medicine or other exposure. / adherence: Extent to which medicine use corresponds with the agreed plan. / verification source: Person, document, or system used to confirm information.
Speaking task: Read Ren's question and Talia's responses using the checked answers. Stress reports, active, unavailable, and review. Switch roles. Repeat the exchange about the outside instruction without adding a dose or restart recommendation. Complete the Ellis transfer, check the key, then read it aloud with Priya clearly named as the receiving owner.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

04Laboratory paperwork and specimen queriesTwo times, two events+

Your goal

Preserve collection and arrival times accurately when a colleague proposes an unnecessary record change.

This lesson in the book, page 31 ↗

A Read the situation

Two times, two events

A requisition records specimen collection at 09:20. The courier sheet records arrival at 10:05. Colleague Ben proposes replacing 09:20 with 10:05 so the paperwork looks consistent. Assistant Imani notices that the fields describe different events. No patient-identity mismatch, specimen-quality problem, or test result is stated. The pair must retain the event labels and source records, clarify any genuinely ambiguous field through the laboratory process, and avoid creating a false collection time. The 45-minute interval alone does not establish specimen acceptability.

Ben: Clinic colleague / Imani: Medical assistant

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which event occurred at 09:20 according to the requisition?

Answer and explanation

D. Collection

The requisition explicitly labels 09:20 as the collection time.

2. What does 10:05 describe?

Answer and explanation

D. Arrival on the courier sheet

The courier sheet records arrival, which is a different event from collection.

3. What does the interval establish by itself?

Answer and explanation

A. A 45-minute difference between the recorded events

The time difference can be calculated, but no test-specific acceptability criteria or result is supplied.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Collection, courier arrival, laboratory receipt, analysis, and result release are distinct events. Do not assume that arrival on a transport sheet means formal laboratory receipt unless the record defines it that way. Always preserve the stated event label.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
requisition
Request document specifying laboratory testing and relevant information.check the requisition
specimen
Material collected for examination or testing.identify the specimen
collection time
Time the specimen was obtained from its source.record the collection time
arrival time
Time an item reached the stated destination.verify the arrival time
receipt time
Time the receiving service recorded receiving the specimen.document the receipt time
courier sheet
Transport record containing delivery or movement information.reconcile the courier sheet
accession number
Identifier assigned by a laboratory to track a case or specimen.confirm the accession number
specimen label
Information attached to the specimen container for identification.verify the specimen label
specimen source
Body site or material origin relevant to the test.record the specimen source
collection method
Technique by which a specimen was obtained.document the collection method
transport interval
Time between specified transport-related events.calculate the transport interval
preanalytical phase
Steps before analysis that can affect laboratory testing.review the preanalytical phase
analytical phase
Stage in which the laboratory examination or measurement is performed.distinguish the analytical phase
postanalytical phase
Steps after analysis, including review and reporting.describe the postanalytical phase
acceptance criteria
Requirements for accepting a specimen or process.check acceptance criteria
rejection criteria
Conditions under which a specimen cannot be accepted for the intended test.apply rejection criteria
specimen integrity
Condition of the specimen relevant to reliable testing.assess specimen integrity
hemolysis
Breakdown of red blood cells that can affect some tests.report hemolysis
insufficient quantity
Amount below what is required for the intended test.report insufficient quantity
transport medium
Material used to preserve or support a specimen during movement.verify the transport medium
temperature condition
Required or recorded temperature circumstances.document temperature conditions
event label
Description of the event to which a time or value belongs.preserve the event label
record amendment
Traceable correction or addition through the approved record process.make a record amendment
turnaround time
Interval between defined start and completion events for a service.define the turnaround time

A 45-minute interval is not itself a specimen-quality verdict. Requirements depend on the specimen, test, container, handling, and actual laboratory procedure. This lesson practices document language, not collection, transport, acceptance, or rejection procedures.

C Notice the language

Question the field, not the colleague

A correction request can be challenged without making the exchange personal. Name the field and event that would change, then propose a read-back. Two different values can both be correct when they refer to different events.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Identify the first field
The requisition says collection at 09:20.
Identify the second field
The courier sheet says arrival at 10:05.
Name the distinction
Those times describe different events.
Question the proposed change
Why would an arrival time replace a collection time?
Keep both facts
We should preserve each time with its event label.
Avoid cosmetic correction
Matching numbers would make the record less accurate here.
Read back the sequence
Collected at 09:20; arrival recorded at 10:05.
Clarify an ambiguous field
Let us ask the laboratory which event this field requires.
Do not invent a receipt event
The sheet says arrival; it does not establish every later laboratory step.
Separate calculation and judgment
The interval is 45 minutes, not an acceptability decision.
Name missing criteria
We do not have the test-specific acceptance criteria here.
Keep identifiers separate
No identity discrepancy is established by these different times.
Preserve the source
Retain the original requisition and courier references.
Use a traceable process
Any genuine correction needs the approved amendment process.
Do not infer a result
The timing records do not tell us the test result.
Close accurately
Both entries can remain accurate when their event labels are preserved.
6 language and precision notes
Says collection
Anchors the value to the source and event.
Different events
Explains why numerical disagreement is not necessarily an error.
Would replace
Makes the consequence of the proposed edit explicit.
Here
Limits the conclusion to the supplied records, not every future discrepancy.
Not an acceptability decision
Separates a calculation from a laboratory judgment.
Any genuine correction
Leaves room for real errors without manufacturing one.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which combined entry preserves the facts?

Answer and explanation

D. Collection 09:20; arrival 10:05

The two original times remain attached to their stated events.

2. Why should 09:20 not be replaced merely for consistency?

Answer and explanation

D. The replacement would change a collection event into an arrival time.

Matching the numbers would falsify the supplied event meaning rather than resolve a real discrepancy.

3. Which conclusion is unsupported?

Answer and explanation

D. The specimen is acceptable because only 45 minutes elapsed.

No test-specific acceptance criteria or relevant handling facts support an acceptability decision.

4. If a destination field is unclear, what is the next step?

Answer and explanation

A. Clarify the required event with the laboratory through the approved process.

The field meaning must be established before transferring a time into it.

E Conversations

Two times, two events

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Ben: Clinic colleague / Imani: Medical assistant

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • Acceptance criteria
  • Receipt time
  • specimen label
  • Turnaround time
  • record amendment
  • requisition
  • collection time
  • arrival time
  • transport interval
  • event label
  1. Ben
    I have two different times on this paperwork. The requisition says 09:20, but the courier sheet says 10:05. Should I make them match?
  2. Imani
    First check the 1. The requisition records collection, while the courier sheet records arrival. Those are not two competing values for the same event.
  3. Ben
    So replacing the earlier time would not simply clean up the paperwork. It would say the specimen was collected later than the requisition records.
  4. Imani
    Exactly. The 2 is 09:20 in that source. Keeping the record accurate matters more than making two boxes look identical.
  5. Ben
    Let me read it back: collected at nine twenty; arrival at ten oh five. The difference is forty-five minutes, but each time has its own meaning.
  6. Imani
    That preserves the 3 correctly. We also should not rename arrival as a later laboratory event unless the record actually defines it that way.
  7. Ben
    I was about to enter ten oh five in a field labeled received. I should check what that field means before assuming the courier entry is enough.
  8. Imani
    Yes. 4 depends on the receiving record and process. Ask the laboratory which event the destination field requires rather than copying a nearby value.
  9. Ben
    The patient details match in these records. I was only looking at the two times; I do not have evidence of a labeling problem.
  10. Imani
    That would be a separate 5 or identification question. No identity discrepancy is established here, so we should not turn an event-time difference into a patient mismatch.
  11. Ben
    A colleague called that a quick delivery and marked the specimen acceptable. We have the journey time, but have we checked the requirements for this test?
  12. Imani
    It gives the 6 between these entries. It does not establish acceptability without the relevant specimen, test, handling conditions, and actual laboratory requirements.
  13. Ben
    Then I will avoid writing acceptable or rejected. Neither result follows just from the times we have read on these two documents.
  14. Imani
    Correct. 7 belong to the real laboratory process. A language clarification about timestamps is not a quality decision.
  15. Ben
    If the laboratory later confirms that a field genuinely was entered incorrectly, we can correct it through the proper process while keeping the history clear.
  16. Imani
    That would be a 8. It should preserve what was changed and why under our requirements, rather than silently overwrite the original source.
  17. Ben
    I also should not tell the patient that these times predict when a result will be ready. Collection-to-arrival is not the entire testing process.
  18. Imani
    Exactly. 9 must have defined start and end points. These entries do not supply an analysis completion or result-release time.
  19. Ben
    I will keep both source references, preserve 09:20 as collection and 10:05 as arrival, and clarify the receiving field before entering anything there.
  20. Imani
    Good. The 10 and courier sheet can both be accurate. Our task is to carry their meanings forward without creating a false match.

Answers and explanations
  1. event label Turn 2. The event label identifies what happened at each time; different events need not have matching timestamps.
  2. collection time Turn 4. The collection time is 09:20 on the requisition and must not be replaced with the separate arrival time.
  3. arrival time Turn 6. The arrival time is 10:05 on the courier sheet; its meaning remains separate from collection and other laboratory events.
  4. Receipt time Turn 8. Receipt time refers to the receiving service's documented event and must not be inferred from an undefined arrival field.
  5. specimen label Turn 10. The specimen label supports identification; the two different event times do not themselves establish an identity mismatch.
  6. transport interval Turn 12. The transport interval is a calculated difference between defined events, not an independent judgment of specimen acceptability.
  7. Acceptance criteria Turn 14. Acceptance criteria are the applicable requirements for the specimen and test; they are not supplied by the time difference alone.
  8. record amendment Turn 16. A record amendment is a traceable correction through the approved process, not an unexplained overwrite to make fields look consistent.
  9. Turnaround time Turn 18. Turnaround time needs defined start and end events; the collection-to-arrival interval does not establish result availability.
  10. requisition Turn 20. The requisition remains the source of the collection entry, while the courier sheet supplies the distinct arrival entry.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Ben: I have two different times on this paperwork. The requisition says 09:20, but the courier sheet says 10:05. Should I make them match?
  2. Imani: First check the event label. The requisition records collection, while the courier sheet records arrival. Those are not two competing values for the same event.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Two times, two events

Fictional paperwork discussion using the lesson case.

  1. Assistant: You suggested changing the collection time to ten oh five. What does that later time describe?
  2. Colleague: It's the courier arrival time. I thought the most recent timestamp should replace the older one.
  3. Assistant: The requisition records collection at nine twenty. Arrival and collection are different events, not competing versions.
  4. Colleague: So the difference doesn't automatically mean someone entered a wrong time on the requisition?
  5. Assistant: Correct. The records can both be accurate because they describe different points in the specimen journey.
  6. Colleague: I'll keep each time with its label. What if someone questions the collection entry itself?
  7. Assistant: Ask the recording team about that entry specifically, without substituting the arrival time.
  8. Colleague: Does the arrival record tell us that the specimen has been accepted for testing?
  9. Assistant: No. Receipt and acceptance are different statuses; we'd need the laboratory's confirmation of acceptance.
  10. Colleague: I'll report collection at nine twenty and arrival at ten oh five, with acceptance unstated.
A request number read back

Fictional clinic-laboratory call; two similar request references need clarification.

  1. Assistant: I'm checking receipt for request L36, but the message I received refers to L63.
  2. Laboratory clerk: Please repeat the reference from your requisition slowly so I can check the correct entry.
  3. Assistant: The requisition says L36. The reply says L63, with those two digits reversed.
  4. Laboratory clerk: Thank you. I can see L36 now; its receipt is recorded, but no result is listed.
  5. Assistant: Then your confirmation concerns L36's receipt, not L63 or a completed test result.
  6. Laboratory clerk: Exactly. I'll correct the reference in my reply so the two enquiries aren't joined together.
  7. Assistant: Could you also identify receipt explicitly? Our clinician asked whether the laboratory had the specimen.
  8. Laboratory clerk: Yes. The reply will say receipt confirmed for L36, with result status not available here.
  9. Assistant: That's what we needed. I won't call the specimen lost based on that reference mismatch.
  10. Laboratory clerk: Thank you for checking. The corrected reference should let your team attach the reply to the right enquiry.
The label question before a conclusion

Fictional specimen paperwork concern: identifiers differ; the laboratory lead must review.

  1. Assistant: The requisition and specimen identifier don't agree. I'm reporting that mismatch before describing test status.
  2. Laboratory lead: Which sources differ, and has anyone confirmed that the sample belongs to the requisition you have?
  3. Assistant: No match has been confirmed. I'm reporting the difference, not deciding which source is correct.
  4. Laboratory lead: Thank you. I'll handle the review with the responsible team; don't resolve identity from memory.
  5. Assistant: The clinician is asking when the result will appear. What can I say about that?
  6. Laboratory lead: Say an identifier discrepancy is under review and no completion time has been established.
  7. Assistant: Has rejection been confirmed, or is that decision still pending?
  8. Laboratory lead: No acceptance or rejection decision has been communicated. Keep those statuses distinct from the review.
  9. Assistant: I'll give that update and identify you as the person reviewing the mismatch with the team.
  10. Laboratory lead: Please do. I'll return the verified status through the clinic's laboratory communication route when available.

Lesson 04: Laboratory paperwork and specimen queries
Original fictional case: A requisition records specimen collection at 09:20. The courier sheet records arrival at 10:05. Colleague Ben proposes replacing 09:20 with 10:05 so the paperwork looks consistent. Assistant Imani notices that the fields describe different events. No patient-identity mismatch, specimen-quality problem, or test result is stated. The pair must retain the event labels and source records, clarify any genuinely ambiguous field through the laboratory process, and avoid creating a false collection time. The 45-minute interval alone does not establish specimen acceptability.
Language workshop: Two times, two events
Communication goal: Preserve collection and arrival times accurately when a colleague proposes an unnecessary record change.
Language guidance: Collection, courier arrival, laboratory receipt, analysis, and result release are distinct events. Do not assume that arrival on a transport sheet means formal laboratory receipt unless the record defines it that way. Always preserve the stated event label.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The requisition says collection at 09:20. / The courier sheet says arrival at 10:05. / Those times describe different events. / Why would an arrival time replace a collection time? / We should preserve each time with its event label. / Matching numbers would make the record less accurate here. / Collected at 09:20; arrival recorded at 10:05. / Let us ask the laboratory which event this field requires. / The sheet says arrival; it does not establish every later laboratory step. / The interval is 45 minutes, not an acceptability decision. / We do not have the test-specific acceptance criteria here. / No identity discrepancy is established by these different times. / Retain the original requisition and courier references. / Any genuine correction needs the approved amendment process. / The timing records do not tell us the test result. / Both entries can remain accurate when their event labels are preserved.
Vocabulary: requisition: Request document specifying laboratory testing and relevant information. / specimen: Material collected for examination or testing. / collection time: Time the specimen was obtained from its source. / arrival time: Time an item reached the stated destination. / receipt time: Time the receiving service recorded receiving the specimen. / courier sheet: Transport record containing delivery or movement information. / accession number: Identifier assigned by a laboratory to track a case or specimen. / specimen label: Information attached to the specimen container for identification. / specimen source: Body site or material origin relevant to the test. / collection method: Technique by which a specimen was obtained. / transport interval: Time between specified transport-related events. / preanalytical phase: Steps before analysis that can affect laboratory testing. / analytical phase: Stage in which the laboratory examination or measurement is performed. / postanalytical phase: Steps after analysis, including review and reporting. / acceptance criteria: Requirements for accepting a specimen or process. / rejection criteria: Conditions under which a specimen cannot be accepted for the intended test. / specimen integrity: Condition of the specimen relevant to reliable testing. / hemolysis: Breakdown of red blood cells that can affect some tests. / insufficient quantity: Amount below what is required for the intended test. / transport medium: Material used to preserve or support a specimen during movement. / temperature condition: Required or recorded temperature circumstances. / event label: Description of the event to which a time or value belongs. / record amendment: Traceable correction or addition through the approved record process. / turnaround time: Interval between defined start and completion events for a service.
Speaking task: Read Ben and Imani's corrected dialogue. Say nine twenty and ten oh five clearly, with collection and arrival attached. Switch roles. Repeat the received-field clarification and the distinction between elapsed time and acceptability. Complete the 08:40 and 09:15 transfer, check the key, and read it aloud without merging the two events.
Partner: Ben: Clinic colleague / Imani: Medical assistant
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: The collection record says 08:40. The courier sheet says arrival 09:15. The destination field meaning is unclear. No acceptability decision is supplied.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. REFERENCE
Course: Medical Assisting English
Professional audience: Medical assistants and outpatient clinic support staff
Scope: All patients, records, times, and cases are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not clinical assessment, triage, prescribing, specimen procedures, legal advice, or professional certification. Duties depend on local law, training, delegation, supervision, and clinic policy. Follow actual clinical and emergency procedures; these dialogues do not establish that any symptom can safely wait. US sources provide context, not universal rules. Use actual dates and required identifiers in real records.
Lesson 04: Laboratory paperwork and specimen queries
Original fictional case: A requisition records specimen collection at 09:20. The courier sheet records arrival at 10:05. Colleague Ben proposes replacing 09:20 with 10:05 so the paperwork looks consistent. Assistant Imani notices that the fields describe different events. No patient-identity mismatch, specimen-quality problem, or test result is stated. The pair must retain the event labels and source records, clarify any genuinely ambiguous field through the laboratory process, and avoid creating a false collection time. The 45-minute interval alone does not establish specimen acceptability.
Language workshop: Two times, two events
Communication goal: Preserve collection and arrival times accurately when a colleague proposes an unnecessary record change.
Language guidance: Collection, courier arrival, laboratory receipt, analysis, and result release are distinct events. Do not assume that arrival on a transport sheet means formal laboratory receipt unless the record defines it that way. Always preserve the stated event label.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The requisition says collection at 09:20. / The courier sheet says arrival at 10:05. / Those times describe different events. / Why would an arrival time replace a collection time? / We should preserve each time with its event label. / Matching numbers would make the record less accurate here. / Collected at 09:20; arrival recorded at 10:05. / Let us ask the laboratory which event this field requires. / The sheet says arrival; it does not establish every later laboratory step. / The interval is 45 minutes, not an acceptability decision. / We do not have the test-specific acceptance criteria here. / No identity discrepancy is established by these different times. / Retain the original requisition and courier references. / Any genuine correction needs the approved amendment process. / The timing records do not tell us the test result. / Both entries can remain accurate when their event labels are preserved.
Vocabulary: requisition: Request document specifying laboratory testing and relevant information. / specimen: Material collected for examination or testing. / collection time: Time the specimen was obtained from its source. / arrival time: Time an item reached the stated destination. / receipt time: Time the receiving service recorded receiving the specimen. / courier sheet: Transport record containing delivery or movement information. / accession number: Identifier assigned by a laboratory to track a case or specimen. / specimen label: Information attached to the specimen container for identification. / specimen source: Body site or material origin relevant to the test. / collection method: Technique by which a specimen was obtained. / transport interval: Time between specified transport-related events. / preanalytical phase: Steps before analysis that can affect laboratory testing. / analytical phase: Stage in which the laboratory examination or measurement is performed. / postanalytical phase: Steps after analysis, including review and reporting. / acceptance criteria: Requirements for accepting a specimen or process. / rejection criteria: Conditions under which a specimen cannot be accepted for the intended test. / specimen integrity: Condition of the specimen relevant to reliable testing. / hemolysis: Breakdown of red blood cells that can affect some tests. / insufficient quantity: Amount below what is required for the intended test. / transport medium: Material used to preserve or support a specimen during movement. / temperature condition: Required or recorded temperature circumstances. / event label: Description of the event to which a time or value belongs. / record amendment: Traceable correction or addition through the approved record process. / turnaround time: Interval between defined start and completion events for a service.
Speaking task: Read Ben and Imani's corrected dialogue. Say nine twenty and ten oh five clearly, with collection and arrival attached. Switch roles. Repeat the received-field clarification and the distinction between elapsed time and acceptability. Complete the 08:40 and 09:15 transfer, check the key, and read it aloud without merging the two events.
Partner: Ben: Clinic colleague / Imani: Medical assistant
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: The collection record says 08:40. The courier sheet says arrival 09:15. The destination field meaning is unclear. No acceptability decision is supplied.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Ben: I also should not tell the patient that these times predict when a result will be ready. Collection-to-arrival is not the entire testing process.
Imani: Exactly. Turnaround time must have defined start and end points. These entries do not supply an analysis completion or result-release time.
Ben: I will keep both source references, preserve 09:20 as collection and 10:05 as arrival, and clarify the receiving field before entering anything there.
Imani: Good. The requisition and courier sheet can both be accurate. Our task is to carry their meanings forward without creating a false match.
END REFERENCE
05Referrals and appointment dependenciesFriday is a status check+

Your goal

Explain referral stages and a named follow-up date without accidentally confirming an appointment.

This lesson in the book, page 40 ↗

A Read the situation

Friday is a status check

Referral F8 was sent Monday and received by the specialist office Tuesday. The office reports that it is under review; no appointment is booked. Coordinator Lia has agreed to check its status Friday. Patient Morgan believes receipt means an appointment on Friday. Assistant Omar must clarify the actual sequence, distinguish a status check from a booking, and identify Lia as the follow-up owner. No review deadline, appointment date, coverage decision, or reason for the review duration is supplied. Clinical concerns require the clinical route, not a wait for this administrative check.

Morgan: Patient / Omar: Medical assistant

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What happened Tuesday?

Answer and explanation

D. The specialist office received referral F8.

Tuesday is the recorded receipt event, not attendance or booking.

2. What is Friday for?

Answer and explanation

A. Lia's status check

The only Friday commitment is Lia checking the referral status.

3. What remains unconfirmed?

Answer and explanation

A. An appointment date

No appointment has been booked in the supplied facts.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Sent, received, reviewed, offered, booked, attended, and reported are different stages. Even referral complete can have different system meanings. State the actual event instead of assuming a status label proves that the patient has been seen.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
referral
Request for another service or professional to assess or provide care.send a referral
referring clinician
Professional initiating a referral.identify the referring clinician
receiving service
Organization or team to which a referral is sent.contact the receiving service
specialist office
Practice or department providing care in a particular specialty.check with the specialist office
referral identifier
Reference used to track a particular referral.quote the referral identifier
sent status
Record that a referral or message was transmitted.verify the sent status
receipt acknowledgment
Confirmation that the receiving organization obtained the referral.obtain a receipt acknowledgment
under review
Being assessed through a process before a further decision or action.explain under review
booking confirmation
Verified notice that an appointment has been scheduled.obtain booking confirmation
appointment availability
Times or capacity potentially available for booking.check appointment availability
referral coordinator
Person organizing referral tracking and related communication.name the referral coordinator
follow-up checkpoint
Agreed point for checking progress or reporting status.set a follow-up checkpoint
dependency
Event or requirement that another step relies on.identify a booking dependency
supporting documentation
Information accompanying a request to support its assessment.supply supporting documentation
referral criteria
Requirements used by a service to assess an appropriate referral.clarify referral criteria
clinical priority
Urgency or relative need determined through clinical assessment.refer clinical-priority questions
waiting list
Record of people awaiting an available service.verify waiting-list status
cancellation slot
Appointment time made available when an existing booking is canceled.check a cancellation slot
appointment offer
Proposed date or time not necessarily accepted or finalized.confirm an appointment offer
attendance
Actual presence at an appointment or service.confirm attendance
referral completion
Completion of the intended referral process, with its definition made clear.document referral completion
consultation report
Account returned after a specialist or other consultation.obtain the consultation report
care coordination
Organization of care activities and information across people or services.support care coordination
access barrier
Practical obstacle to obtaining an intended service.identify an access barrier

A Friday status check is not a Friday appointment or a clinical recommendation to wait until Friday. Keep administrative tracking separate from urgent clinical needs. A named coordinator improves follow-up without controlling the specialist decision or appointment availability.

C Notice the language

Explain the stage in ordinary language

A word such as received may sound final to someone outside the clinic. Give the plain-language meaning and the next known action. Repeating under review without explanation can sound evasive; inventing a likely appointment can be misleading.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Identify the referral
We are discussing referral F8.
State transmission
It was sent Monday.
State receipt
The specialist office received it Tuesday.
Explain the stage
They report that it is under review.
Correct the appointment inference
No appointment has been booked.
Define Friday
Friday is Lia's status check, not your appointment.
Name the owner
Lia has agreed to check the status.
Avoid a review guarantee
We do not have a confirmed review-completion date.
Keep an offer distinct
An offered time still needs the relevant booking confirmation.
Do not invent a cause
I do not have a verified reason for the review duration.
Preserve the next step
Lia will check what stage the referral has reached.
Route clinical concern
A new or urgent concern needs our clinical route.
Clarify access needs
We can communicate relevant access needs through the proper process.
Avoid a coverage claim
Referral receipt does not itself confirm insurance coverage.
Check understanding
The referral is received and under review, with no booked appointment.
Close precisely
The confirmed next action is Lia's Friday status check.
6 language and precision notes
They report
Attributes the status to the receiving office.
No appointment
Explicitly corrects the patient's central misunderstanding.
Not your appointment
Prevents a follow-up day from becoming an arrival instruction.
Has agreed
Identifies a real owner rather than a vague someone.
Confirmed next action
Offers certainty about the known task without controlling external decisions.
New or urgent concern
Keeps clinical needs separate from administrative timing.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which update accurately uses all three days?

Answer and explanation

B. Sent Monday, received Tuesday, and Lia checks status Friday; no appointment is booked.

Each day is attached to its actual event, and the absence of a booking remains explicit.

2. Which sentence creates a false expectation?

Answer and explanation

D. We will see you at the specialist on Friday.

Friday is only a coordinator checkpoint, not a supplied appointment.

3. What does receipt establish?

Answer and explanation

C. The referral reached the receiving office, not that a slot is booked.

Receipt is an earlier administrative stage and does not prove later decisions or attendance.

4. How should a new clinical concern be handled?

Answer and explanation

B. Through the clinic's appropriate clinical route rather than waiting for Lia's routine check

Administrative follow-up timing does not establish clinical safety or replace assessment.

Friday is a status check

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Morgan: Patient / Omar: Medical assistant

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • appointment offer
  • care coordination
  • receiving service
  • referral coordinator
  • clinical priority
  • Under review
  • booking confirmation
  • follow-up checkpoint
  • referral identifier
  • receipt acknowledgment
  1. Morgan
    I heard the specialist received my referral on Tuesday, and someone mentioned Friday. What time should I arrive for the appointment?
  2. Omar
    Let me clarify the 1. Friday is when Lia will check its status. No specialist appointment has been booked.
  3. Morgan
    I thought received meant they had accepted me into a slot. Is there still another step before I can arrange time away from work?
  4. Omar
    The 2 means the office received the referral. They say it is under review, and we do not yet have a date for you to attend.
  5. Morgan
    Let me get this straight. It went out Monday and reached them Tuesday. What exactly is Lia doing on Friday if I am not booked in?
  6. Omar
    The 3 is F8. It was sent Monday, received Tuesday, and Lia has agreed to check the status Friday.
  7. Morgan
    Does under review mean the referral has been rejected, or that the office is still deciding what happens next?
  8. Omar
    4 is the status they gave us. We should not turn it into either a rejection or an appointment confirmation without further information.
  9. Morgan
    Who is Lia in this process? I need to know whether she is the person checking the status or the clinician deciding about my care.
  10. Omar
    Lia is the 5 handling this follow-up. Her Friday check does not control the specialist's decision or guarantee that their review will finish that day.
  11. Morgan
    I nearly asked for Friday off work. Please make the next message clear about whether it is an update or an actual appointment offer.
  12. Omar
    Correct. A 6 is different from the status check. At present, no appointment date or time is confirmed.
  13. Morgan
    If they offer a time later, I may need to explain my work schedule and transport arrangements. I cannot assume every offered slot is possible.
  14. Omar
    An 7 should be handled through the booking process. Relevant access needs can be communicated appropriately rather than assuming an offered time has already been accepted.
  15. Morgan
    I also wondered whether the referral being received means my plan has approved the visit. I do not want to confuse the two processes.
  16. Omar
    The 8 receiving a referral does not itself confirm coverage. Any coverage question needs the appropriate verification, separate from the current referral status.
  17. Morgan
    What if the concern I was referred for changes before Friday? Should I simply wait because that is when Lia has promised to check?
  18. Omar
    No administrative checkpoint establishes that waiting is safe. A change or urgent concern needs our clinical route for 9, not an assumption based on Lia's diary.
  19. Morgan
    Understood. The referral reached them Tuesday and remains under review. Lia will check Friday, but I do not have an appointment yet.
  20. Omar
    Exactly. That is the current 10 position. I will keep the status and ownership clear, and any actual booking information must be confirmed separately.

Answers and explanations
  1. follow-up checkpoint Turn 2. The follow-up checkpoint is Lia's Friday status check, not a booked time for Morgan to attend the specialist.
  2. receipt acknowledgment Turn 4. Receipt acknowledgment confirms the referral reached the specialist office; it does not establish an appointment or completed clinical review.
  3. referral identifier Turn 6. The referral identifier F8 links the conversation to the specific request, preserving which case the three dates describe.
  4. Under review Turn 8. Under review describes the current assessment stage; it is not a supplied rejection or confirmed booking.
  5. referral coordinator Turn 10. The referral coordinator owns the administrative status check here, not the specialist's clinical decision or appointment availability.
  6. booking confirmation Turn 12. Booking confirmation would establish a scheduled appointment, which is specifically absent from the supplied referral status.
  7. appointment offer Turn 14. An appointment offer is a proposed time that still needs the relevant agreement and confirmation, not automatic attendance.
  8. receiving service Turn 16. The receiving service's acknowledgment concerns referral receipt and does not by itself verify the patient's insurance coverage.
  9. clinical priority Turn 18. Clinical priority requires the appropriate clinical assessment and cannot be inferred from a routine coordinator follow-up date.
  10. care coordination Turn 20. Care coordination keeps the referral status, owner, and next action connected without falsely reporting that the specialist visit is booked.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Morgan: I heard the specialist received my referral on Tuesday, and someone mentioned Friday. What time should I arrive for the appointment?
  2. Omar: Let me clarify the follow-up checkpoint. Friday is when Lia will check its status. No specialist appointment has been booked.
  3. Morgan: I thought received meant they had accepted me into a slot. Is there still another step before I can arrange time away from work?
  4. Omar: The receipt acknowledgment means the office received the referral. They say it is under review, and we do not yet have a date for you to attend.
  5. Morgan: Let me get this straight. It went out Monday and reached them Tuesday. What exactly is Lia doing on Friday if I am not booked in?
  6. Omar: The referral identifier is F8. It was sent Monday, received Tuesday, and Lia has agreed to check the status Friday.
  7. Morgan: Does under review mean the referral has been rejected, or that the office is still deciding what happens next?
  8. Omar: Under review is the status they gave us. We should not turn it into either a rejection or an appointment confirmation without further information.
  9. Morgan: Who is Lia in this process? I need to know whether she is the person checking the status or the clinician deciding about my care.
  10. Omar: Lia is the referral coordinator handling this follow-up. Her Friday check does not control the specialist's decision or guarantee that their review will finish that day.
  11. Morgan: I nearly asked for Friday off work. Please make the next message clear about whether it is an update or an actual appointment offer.
  12. Omar: Correct. A booking confirmation is different from the status check. At present, no appointment date or time is confirmed.
  13. Morgan: If they offer a time later, I may need to explain my work schedule and transport arrangements. I cannot assume every offered slot is possible.
  14. Omar: An appointment offer should be handled through the booking process. Relevant access needs can be communicated appropriately rather than assuming an offered time has already been accepted.
  15. Morgan: I also wondered whether the referral being received means my plan has approved the visit. I do not want to confuse the two processes.
  16. Omar: The receiving service receiving a referral does not itself confirm coverage. Any coverage question needs the appropriate verification, separate from the current referral status.
  17. Morgan: What if the concern I was referred for changes before Friday? Should I simply wait because that is when Lia has promised to check?
  18. Omar: No administrative checkpoint establishes that waiting is safe. A change or urgent concern needs our clinical route for clinical priority, not an assumption based on Lia's diary.
  19. Morgan: Understood. The referral reached them Tuesday and remains under review. Lia will check Friday, but I do not have an appointment yet.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Friday is a check, not a visit

Fictional referral update using the lesson case.

  1. Patient: I took Friday off for the specialist. What time should I arrive there?
  2. Assistant: Friday is Lia's status check, not a booked visit. I'm sorry that distinction wasn't clear.
  3. Patient: But the specialist office received the referral Tuesday. Doesn't that mean they accepted an appointment?
  4. Assistant: Receipt means it arrived. They say it's under review, with no appointment booked.
  5. Patient: Was sending it Monday a separate step, or did Tuesday replace that date?
  6. Assistant: Both are relevant: sent Monday, received Tuesday. Neither date is a visit confirmation.
  7. Patient: Please tell Lia I've arranged leave and need the actual date before making travel plans.
  8. Assistant: I'll pass that on without promising the review will finish before Friday.
  9. Patient: Should I call the specialist to ask which site I should attend anyway?
  10. Assistant: We don't have a booked site yet. The appointment confirmation will need both location and time.
The same clinic name at two sites

Fictional confirmation lists East Clinic; patient assumes the West site.

  1. Assistant: Your confirmation says East Clinic on Thursday at ten. Which site were you planning to visit?
  2. Patient: West Clinic, because that's where I went last year for another appointment.
  3. Assistant: This confirmation names East. Last year's location doesn't establish the site for Thursday.
  4. Patient: Could you read the street name from the confirmation so I can check transport?
  5. Assistant: It says Lake Street. I'll repeat the full location from your current confirmation privately.
  6. Patient: Thank you. I thought East and West were departments in the same building.
  7. Assistant: They are listed as different sites here. I won't describe them as one building.
  8. Patient: Does checking the address change the time or create a second appointment for me?
  9. Assistant: No, we are clarifying the existing Thursday booking, not changing it or creating another.
  10. Patient: Then I'll plan for East Clinic on Lake Street at ten, as this confirmation says.
Receipt not found at the receiving office

Fictional referral has a sent record but no acknowledgment.

  1. Assistant: Our record says referral H4 was sent yesterday, but the specialist office can't find it.
  2. Referral coordinator: Do we have an acknowledgment, or only the record of our outgoing submission?
  3. Assistant: Only the outgoing record. I haven't described receipt as confirmed to the patient.
  4. Referral coordinator: I'll check the destination details and contact the receiving office about that reference.
  5. Assistant: Could you tell me what wording to use while your delivery enquiry remains open?
  6. Referral coordinator: Say we have a sent record but haven't confirmed receipt. Avoid calling the referral reviewed.
  7. Assistant: The patient asks whether a new referral is required. No clinician has decided that.
  8. Referral coordinator: Keep that decision separate. We're first checking where the existing request went.
  9. Assistant: I'll explain the delivery enquiry and say you are coordinating it with the receiving office.
  10. Referral coordinator: Please do. I'll report what they confirm rather than simply resend and assume the problem is solved.
